\mathchoice{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}(v)
are the components of the symmetric optical tidal matrix introduced in (
31
). They are composed of the Ricci curvature,
\Phi_{00}
, and the Weyl curvature,
\Psi_{0}
.

Then, we understand that the Lagrangian function in (
49
) is reduced by two degrees of freedom. In that case, the geodesic deviation action can be rewritten entirely through the screen projections of the deviation vector and their derivatives with respect to the affine parameter
v
, i.e.,

S_{\xi}=\int L_{\rm{red}}\,dv,

(51)

with
L_{\rm{red}}
given in (
50
).

Since we are interested in a process of null dust emission from a source to an observer, we are only interested in those Lorentz transformations that keep the propagation direction unchanged. Those types of transformations are known as the Type-I Lorentz transformations within the context of the Newman-Penrose formalism
[
110
]
and they are given through

\displaystyle\vec{k}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\vec{k},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\vec{m}\rightarrow f\vec{k}+\vec{m},

\displaystyle\vec{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow f\bar{f}\vec{k}+\vec{n}+\bar{f}\vec{m}+f\vec{\bar{m}},\qquad\qquad\vec{\bar{m}}\rightarrow\bar{f}\vec{k}+\vec{\bar{m}}.

(52)

Here,
f
is an arbitrary complex scalar and
\{\vec{k},\vec{n},\vec{m},\vec{\bar{m}}\}
complete the semi-null tetrad of Newman and Penrose. The only non-vanishing inner products associated with this tetrad are

\langle\vec{k},\vec{n}\rangle=-1
and
\langle\vec{m},\vec{\bar{m}}\rangle=1
where
\{\vec{m},\vec{\bar{m}}\}
span the screen-basis. They are related to the (parallel-propagated) Sachs basis via
\vec{m}=\left(\vec{s_{1}}-i\vec{s_{2}}\right)/\sqrt{2}
and
\vec{\bar{m}}=\left(\vec{s_{1}}+i\vec{s_{2}}\right)/\sqrt{2}
up to a
v
-independent 2-dimensional rotation. It is known that
[
135
]

\Phi_{00}\rightarrow\Phi_{00}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\Psi_{0}\rightarrow\Psi_{0},

(53)

under the transformation given in (
52
). Then, it is easy to show that, under Type-I Lorentz transformations,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}\dot{\xi}^{a}\dot{\xi}^{b}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}{\delta^{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{a}{b}}}\dot{\xi}^{a}\dot{\xi}^{b},\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\mathchoice{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}\xi^{a}\xi^{b}

(54)

holds.
The second relation in the above follows from the definition of the optical tidal matrix, (
34
), and the transformation properties of the Ricci and Weyl scalars given in (
53
).

Thus, we conclude that the Lagrangian, (
50
), and the action functional, (
51
), of the system are invariant under the Lorentz transformations that keep the propagation direction unchanged.

In the next subsection, we give a summary of the Hamiltonian formulation of the null bundle dynamics defined on a reduced symplectic phase space in
[
145
]
.

3.3
Symplectic evolution in phase space

We reminded in the previous subsection that the action functional of the null geodesic deviation can be reduced by two dimensions in the case where the deviation vector and the propagation vector are orthogonal to each other. Then, the Lagrangian is composed of only the screen projections of the deviation vector and their derivatives along the propagation. In this subsection we give a summary of the Hamiltonian dynamics to study the evolution of a bundle on a reduced phase space.

In our previous work
[
145
]
, we defined a
4
-dimensional phase space vector via the following Darboux coordinates,

\mathbf{z}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c }q^{a}\\
p_{b}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c }\xi^{{1}}\\
\xi^{{2}}\\
\dot{\xi}_{{1}}\\
\dot{\xi}_{{2}}\end{array}\right].

(55)

Here, the screen basis components,
\bm{\xi}
, of the deviation vector act as canonical coordinates and their derivatives,
\bm{\dot{\xi}}
, act as canonical momenta.

The Hamiltonian of the system, corresponding to the reduced Lagrangian was found through

H=p_{a}\dot{q}^{a}-L_{\rm{red}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathchoice{\delta^{{a}{b}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}{\delta^{{a}{b}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}{\delta^{{a}{b}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}{\delta^{{a}{b}}_{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\dot{\xi}_{a}\dot{\xi}_{b}+\mathcal{V}\left(\bm{\xi},v\right),

(56)

with

\mathcal{V}\left(\bm{\xi},v\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathchoice{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}\xi^{a}\xi^{b},

(57)

which is analogous to a time dependent Hamiltonian of a classical oscillator. In our case, the evolution parameter is the affine parameter,
v
, and we refer to the term
\mathcal{V}\left(\bm{\xi},v\right)
as the
ray bundle potential
. Due to the linearity of the problem, the Hamiltonian function was rewritten as,

H\left(\mathbf{z},v\right)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{z}^{\intercal}\bm{\Omega}^{\intercal}\mathbf{H}\,\mathbf{z},\qquad{\rm{where}}\qquad\mathbf{H}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{0_{2}}&\,\,\delta^{ab}\\
\hline\cr\mathchoice{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}{\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.16666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}_{{\,}{a}{b}}}&\,\,\mathbf{0_{2}}\end{array}\right],

(58)

is the Hamiltonian matrix of the system. It satisfies
\mathbf{\Omega}{\mathbf{{H}}}=\left(\mathbf{\Omega}{\mathbf{{H}}}\right)^{\intercal}
due to the optical tidal matrix,
\bm{\mathcal{R}}
, being symmetric. Matrix
\mathbf{\Omega}
is known as the
standard/fundamental symplectic matrix
in the literature
[
37
,
141
]
, whose components are given by,

††
††

††

Matrices
\mathbf{0_{2}}
and
\mathbf{I_{2}}
refer to the
2
-dimensional zero and identity matrices.

\Omega^{ij}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{0_{2}}&\,\,\mathbf{I_{2}}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{-I_{2}}&\,\,\mathbf{0_{2}}\end{array}\right],\qquad{\rm{with}}\qquad\bm{\Omega}^{\intercal}=\bm{\Omega}^{-1}=-\bm{\Omega},\qquad\bm{\Omega}^{2}=-\mathbf{I_{4}},\qquad{\rm{det}}\bm{\Omega}=1,

(59)

where “
\rm{det}
” refers to the determinant of a matrix. The Hamiltonian equations were written as a matrix equation,

\dot{\mathbf{z}}=\mathbf{H}\,\mathbf{z},

(60)

whose solution is given by a linear transformation

\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{T}\left(v,v_{0}\right)\mathbf{z_{0}}.

(61)

The
4\times 4
transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, was obtained by an ordered exponential (OE) map of the Hamiltonian matrix,
{\mathbf{{H}}}
, i.e.,

\mathbf{T}\left(v,v_{0}\right)={\rm{OE}}\left[{\int_{v_{0}}^{v}{\mathbf{{H}}}dv}\right],\qquad{\rm{with}}\qquad\mathbf{T}\left(v_{0},v_{0}\right)=\mathbf{I_{4}}.\\

In
[
145
]
, we put
\mathbf{T}
in a block form by making use of
2\times 2
submatrices
\{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}\}
, i.e,

\mathbf{T}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{B}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{C}&\mathbf{D}\end{array}\right],

(62)

in order to emphasize the analogy between the symplectic
ABCD
transformation matrices of the Newtonian paraxial optics
[
139
]
and our ray bundle transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
. We should mention that this matrix was previously discovered in
[
50
]
via a Wrońskian method in order to solve the geodesic deviation equation for arbitrary initial conditions without the realization of the underlying Hamiltonian structure. It was also used in other applications in order to propagate light in realistic scenarios
[
51
]
.

In our previous work, we realized that the light bundle transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, satisfies
[
145
]

\mathbf{T}^{\intercal}\,\mathbf{\Omega}\,\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{\Omega},\qquad{\rm{with}}\qquad\rm{det}\,\mathbf{T}=1,

(63)

as exponential map of Hamiltonian matrices are symplectic matrices.
The symplecticity condition (
63
) imply that

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal},\,\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\mathbf{C}},\,\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\mathbf{D}}\,\,\rm{and}\,\,\mathbf{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{\mathbf{D}}^{\intercal}\,\,\rm{are\,\,symmetric,\,and}\,\,\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\mathbf{D}}^{\intercal}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\mathbf{C}}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{I_{2}}.

(64)

Moreover, the Hamiltonian equations, (
60
), are equivalent to,

\displaystyle\mathbf{\dot{A}}=\mathbf{C},\qquad\mathbf{\dot{C}}=\bm{\mathcal{R}}\mathbf{A},\qquad\mathbf{A}\left(v_{0},v_{0}\right)=\mathbf{I_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{C}\left(v_{0},v_{0}\right)=\mathbf{0_{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\dot{B}}=\mathbf{D},\qquad\mathbf{\dot{D}}=\bm{\mathcal{R}}\mathbf{B},\qquad\mathbf{B}\left(v_{0},v_{0}\right)=\mathbf{0_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{D}\left(v_{0},v_{0}\right)=\mathbf{I_{2}}.

(65)

due to (
58
) and (
61
).

In this subsection, we outlined the phase space dynamics and the Hamiltonian formulation of the light bundle propagation. We reminded that the screen-projected geodesic deviation variables can be linearly transformed from an initial point to a final point via a symplectic matrix,
\mathbf{T}
.

In the next subsection, we summarize the procedure to reach the Hamilton-Jacobi equation. Note that this is pivotal in the construction of the main body of the paper where we introduce the so-called paraxial wave equation of the bundle.

3.4
Hamilton-Jacobi equation of the ray bundle

In this subsection, we summarize the relationship between the action functional of the null geodesic deviation and the Hamilton-Jacobi equation. In order to achieve this, for our generic canonical transformations,

\displaystyle\bm{\xi^{\prime}}\rightarrow\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}(\bm{\xi^{\prime}},\bm{\dot{\xi^{\prime}}};v),\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\bm{\dot{\xi^{\prime}}}\rightarrow\bm{\dot{\xi}}=\bm{\dot{\xi}}(\bm{\xi^{\prime}},\bm{\dot{\xi}^{\prime}};v),

(66)

we assume that

{\rm{det}}\left[\frac{\partial\left(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}}\right)}{\partial\left(\bm{\dot{\xi^{\prime}}},\bm{\xi^{\prime}}\right)}\right]={\rm{det}}\left[\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial\bm{\dot{\xi}^{\prime}}}\right]={\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}\neq 0,

(67)

holds. Matrix
\mathbf{B}
in the above is the upper-right block of the symplectic ray bundle transfer matrix,
\mathbf{T}
. The physical meaning of this assumption will become clear in a while. For the time being, we can take it as a mathematical assumption which guarantees that our ray bundle transformation is a
free canonical transformation

[
10
]
. The associated
1
-form,
d{S}(\bm{\dot{\xi}^{\prime}},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)=\bm{\dot{\xi}^{\prime}}d\bm{\xi^{\prime}}-\bm{\dot{\xi}}d\bm{\xi}
,
of the transformation given in (
66
) is exact. Then, it can be locally expressed as
{S}(\bm{\dot{\xi}},\bm{\xi};v)\rightarrow S(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)

via a Legendre transformation where
S(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)
is given by

S(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)=\int_{\bm{\xi^{\prime}},v_{0}}^{\bm{\xi},v}\bm{\dot{\xi}}d\bm{\xi}-Hdv.

(68)

Note that the action given in (
68
) is exactly equal to the reduced action,
S_{{\xi}}
, of the ray bundle given in (
51
). Therefore,
S=S_{\xi}
is indeed the generating function of our free canonical transformation and from now on, we will drop the suffix
ξ
from the action functional of the geodesic deviation.

It can also be shown that for the corresponding free canonical transformation, the quadratic generating function
S(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)
can be written by matrix inner products as
[
105
,
36
]

\displaystyle S(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\bm{\xi}-\bm{\xi^{\prime}}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\bm{\xi}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\xi^{\prime}}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{A}\bm{\xi^{\prime}}.

In the Appendix of
[
145
]
, we provided the explicit calculations to show that
S(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)
satisfies

\bm{\dot{\xi}}=\frac{\partial S}{\partial\bm{\xi}},\qquad\bm{\dot{\xi}^{\prime}}=-\frac{\partial S}{\partial\bm{\xi}^{\prime}},

(70)

and the Hamilton-Jacobi equation

\frac{\partial S}{\partial v}+H=0.

(71)

This is expected from any such generating function of a free linear canonical transformation
[
36
]
. Similar proofs can also be found in other works in which one focuses on the linear canonical transforms of systems guided by quadratic Hamiltonians.

Let us now return to assumption (
67
) and its physical relevance.
Recall that the angular diameter distance,
{D}_{A}
, and the luminosity distance,
{D}_{L}
, between an emitter and an observer are obtained via taking the ratios of cross-sectional areas and solid angles at the source and the observation points. For example, one can estimate the cross-sectional area at point
s
via the measured solid angle at a vertex point
o
. Then, the linear canonical transform (symplectomorphysm) one needs to consider is

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{\xi}\\
\bm{\dot{\xi}}\end{array}\right]_{s}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathbf{A}&\,\,\mathbf{B}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{C}&\,\,\mathbf{D}\end{array}\right]_{(v_{s},v_{o})}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{0}\\
\bm{\dot{\xi}}\end{array}\right]_{o},\,

(72)

in order the calculate the angular diameter distance. Note that the initial deviation vector,
\vec{\xi^{\prime}}=\vec{\xi}|_{o}
, is zero in that case as the observer is located at the vertex of the bundle. Then, the angular diameter distance is obtained via
[
120
,
145
]

{D}_{A}=\omega_{o}{\rm{det}}\left|\mathbf{B}\left(v_{s},v_{o}\right)\right|^{1/2}.

(73)

Likewise, one can reverse the situation and consider the case where the bundle is initiated at the source location. Then, the luminosity distance is given by

{D}_{L}=\omega_{s}{\rm{det}}\left|\mathbf{B}\left(v_{o},v_{s}\right)\right|^{1/2}.

(74)

The frequency factors in (
73
) and (
74
) appear due to the solid angles being defined with respect to proper length as in (
46
). Namely, one needs to use the relationship
\omega dv=dl
in order to use the correct parameterization in the definition of the solid angle
‡‡
‡‡

‡‡

We should also note that, matrix
\mathbf{B}
is often denoted by the letter
\bm{\mathcal{D}}
in the literature and it is referred to as the Jacobi matrix. This is mainly because of the fact that only bundles starting from a vertex point are considered in the standard cosmological distance calculations. The physical importance of the total ray bundle transfer matrix and its extensions were appreciated only in a few studies
[
50
,
51
,
88
,
63
,
131
,
87
]
.
.

Now, it is clear that once the determinant of
\mathbf{B}
becomes zero, the cross-sectional area of the bundle collapses to a point or a line and the distance estimations become impossible. Therefore, by assuming that
{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}\neq 0
, one guarantees that there are no caustic points throughout propagation of the null bundle
[
120
]
. This is equivalent to the free canonical transform criterion given in (
67
) which leads one to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
71
).

Here, we emphasize two things: (i) the caustics in question are the ones of an instantaneous wavefront rather than the global wavefront. The caustics of the latter is defined through the set of all points where the null cone ceases to be an immersion, i.e., a submanifold of the spacetime manifold. In that case, one needs to study the entire celestial sphere of the observer and its conjugate points. In our case, we are interested in only a small section of the wavefront and accordingly the criterion
{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}=0
is sufficient to study the local effects. (ii) In general relativity, light bundles are non-twisting at the geometric optics limit. For generic null bundles that do not represent light, twist prevents the cross-sectional area to become zero
[
120
]
. In that case
{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}=0
would not provide the sufficient condition to define the caustics of the bundle in question.

In this section, we gave a brief summary of
[
145
]
in which the dynamics of a thin null bundle was investigated on a reduced phase space. We outlined that the screen projections of the deviation vector act as canonical coordinates and their derivatives act as canonical momenta. We summarized the underlying Hamiltonian formalism of this linear system and showed how to obtain the Hamilton-Jacobi equation by standard techniques.

In the next section, we present the main body of the paper which is essentially constructed on the correspondence between the linear canonical transformations of the null bundle and their associated metaplectic operators. We later show that this methodology allows one to define Gaussian beams on generic curved spacetimes which avoid caustics of the standard null bundles.

4
Wavization of an observed light beam

In this section, we aim to answer the following question. Can one recover some of the wave-like properties of a light beam starting from its geometric optics limit?
In fact, a similar question was asked within the Newtonian optics community decades ago for light propagation in inhomogeneous refractive media
[
32
,
102
,
133
]
.
The main idea behind the answer to such question was to use the analogies between classical optics and quantum mechanics
[
43
,
42
]
.

The procedure of obtaining a classical wave function by making use of quantum mechanical techniques is sometimes referred to as
wavization
in the Newtonian optics community
[
25
,
139
]
. Most methods focus on phase space formulations in the paraxial regime. In those studies, the main focus is on the linear part of the propagation as light passes through an optical device or a medium. The corresponding evolution equation is the paraxial wave equation which takes the same form as the Schrödinger equation
[
55
,
85
,
84
,
7
,
8
,
13
,
154
,
139
,
38
]
. The time independent version is known as the paraxial Helmholtz equation. Those equations represent only classical effects and they do not refer to any quantum behavior.

Here, we present a similar work for light propagation on a generic curved spacetime. The aim is to recover some of the wave-like properties of a light beam by using some phase space quantization techniques.
In our approach, we adhere to the previous assumptions: (i) we focus on only thin light bundles, (ii) we consider sources of monochromatic light.

4.1
The operator formalism

The operator formalism of quantum mechanics has been adapted by the Newtonian optics community and it has been used in many applications
[
109
,
102
,
139
]
.
We follow a similar procedure here.

In Section
3
, we presented a summary of
[
145
]
in which 2-dimensional screen components,
\bm{\xi}
, of the deviation vector, and their derivatives,
\bm{\dot{\xi}}
, play the role of canonical coordinates and momenta, respectively. This choice follows from the fact that the physical observables such as the size and the shape of a bundle, the wavefront curvature, power contained in the bundle or the distance estimations reside solely on the observational screen-projections of the deviation vector and its derivatives. The dyad basis of the observational screen is known as the Sachs basis,
\vec{s}_{a}
, with
\{a,b\}=\{1,2\}
. This basis is parallel propagated along the propagation direction,
\vec{k}
. As we chose to construct a phase space via the dyad basis components, the components
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\dot{\xi}}
are raised and lowered by a 2-dimensional Kronecker delta. This means that the Lagrangian sub-spaces of the phase phase is flat and the mathematical problem is analogous to the one of a 2-dimensional oscillator in classical mechanics.

Let us now define the phase space operators of our system. We start with the ones associated with the canonical coordinates,
\bm{\xi}
, and the canonical momenta,
\bm{\dot{\xi}}
. We define them through the Schrödinger operators acting on
\mathcal{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of square-integrable functions. To be more specific, they act on an arbitrary function
f
, as

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\xi}}\left[{f}\right]:={f}\,\cdot\,\bm{\xi},\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\hat{\bm{\dot{\xi}}}\left[{f}\right]:=-i\bm{\nabla}f,\qquad{\rm{with}}\qquad\bm{\nabla}:=\frac{\partial\,}{\partial\bm{\xi}}.

(75)

Here,
\bm{\nabla}
represents the gradient operator with respect to the screen components of the deviation vector. Those operators satisfy the commutation rule
\left[\hat{\dot{\xi}}_{a},\,\hat{{\xi}}_{b}\right]=-i\delta_{ab}
.
In order to obtain the Hamiltonian operator, we replace the canonical coordinates and momenta in the Hamiltonian function, (
58
), with their corresponding operators given in (
75
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{H}\left(\mathbf{z},t\right)=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\mathbf{z}}^{\dagger}\bm{\Omega}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{H}}\hat{\mathbf{z}},\qquad{\rm{with}}\qquad\hat{\mathbf{z}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c }\hat{\bm{\xi}}\\
\hat{\bm{\dot{\xi}}}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c }\hat{\xi}^{{1}}\\
\hat{\xi}^{{2}}\\
\hat{\dot{\xi}}_{{1}}\\
\hat{\dot{\xi}}_{{2}}\end{array}\right],

where “
{\dagger}
” denotes the conjugate transpose operator.
Note that as we are considering only quadratic Hamiltonians, there is no ordering problem here. The Hamiltonian operator in (
4.1
) can be written more explicitly as

\hat{H}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{\dot{\xi}}}^{\dagger}\hat{\bm{\dot{\xi}}}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger}\mathbf{\mathcal{R}}\hat{\bm{\xi}},

(82)

in which
\mathbf{\mathcal{R}}
is the symmetric optical tidal matrix as in (
34
) that incorporates the information about the spacetime curvature along the propagation direction.

4.2
Propagator of the wave function associated with the light beam

In the null bundle approach, the information about the trajectory of each geodesic in the bundle is coarse-grained. Here, we assign a wave function to this coarse-grained system of particles. Accordingly, the wave function can be understood as an effective wave function associated with a collection of non-interacting particles, i.e., the null dust.
In the previous subsection, we presented the Schrödinger operators of the phase space. Those will be used in the evolution equation of the light beam wave function in the current section.

Recall from Section
3
that there exists a
4\times 4
symplectic ray bundle transfer matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, that takes an initial phase space vector,
\mathbf{z}_{0}
, to a final one,
\mathbf{z}
. Those matrices form a symplectic group which we denote as
\rm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
.
We now introduce the metaplectic group,
\rm{Mp}(4,\mathbb{R})
, which is the unitary representation of the double cover of
\rm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})

[
149
]
. This means that for every ray bundle transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, there exist two unitary operators belonging to
\rm{Mp}(4,\mathbb{R})
which differ by a sign
[
105
,
92
]
. Metaplectic operators,
\hat{U}
, act on the space of square integrable functions and they are viewed as generalized quadratic Fourier transformers. Accordingly, they act as propagators for our square integrable wave functions.

Following
[
153
]
, let us consider the integral transform

\Uppsi\left(\bm{\xi},v\right)=\hat{U}(\mathbf{T})[\Uppsi_{0}]:=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Uppsi_{0}\left(\bm{\xi^{\prime}},v^{\prime}\right)K\left(\bm{\xi},v;\bm{\xi^{\prime}},v^{\prime}\right)d^{2}\bm{\xi}^{\prime}.

(83)

Here, the metaplectic operator,
\hat{U}(\mathbf{T})
, associated with the ray bundle transfer matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, acts as a propagator of the wave function along
\vec{k}
.
Namely, it is represented by a kernel,
K\left(\bm{\xi},v;\bm{\xi^{\prime}},v^{\prime}\right)
, which propagates an initial wave function,
\Uppsi_{0}\left(\bm{\xi}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\right)
, to a final one,
\Uppsi\left(\bm{\xi},v\right)
. Let us recall one more time that the evolution parameter in question is the affine parameter,
v
, that parametrizes the integral curves of
\vec{k}
in this work. The explicit form of the kernel can be obtained through the semi-classical method of Van Vleck
[
147
]
, Morette
[
104
]
and Van Hove
[
146
]
(See also
[
30
]
.). For a system defined on a 4-dimensional phase space, it takes the following form

K\left(\bm{\xi},v;\bm{\xi^{\prime}},v^{\prime}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\right)|\Delta|^{1/2}\exp{\left(iS\right)},\qquad{\rm{where}}\qquad\Delta={\rm{det}}\left[-\frac{\partial^{2}S}{\partial\bm{\xi}\partial\bm{\xi}^{\prime}}\right]

(84)

is known as the
Van Vleck determinant
. Note that in our case,
S
corresponds to the action functional of the geodesic deviation vector which can be locally expressed through
\bm{\xi}
and
\bm{\xi^{\prime}}
.
Then, substitution of

S(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi^{\prime}};v)=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\bm{\xi}-\bm{\xi^{\prime}}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\bm{\xi}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\xi^{\prime}}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{A}\bm{\xi^{\prime}},

given in (
3.4
) into the kernel in (
84
) allows us to write it explicitly through the submatrices of the ray bundle transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
. It follows as

K\left(\bm{\xi},v;\bm{\xi}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left|{\rm{det}\left(\mathbf{B}^{-1}\right)}\right|^{1/2}\exp{\left[iS\left(\bm{\xi},\bm{\xi}^{\prime},v\right)\right]}.

(85)

In the Newtonian optics, the kernel analogous to the one in (
85
) is known after Collins
[
32
]
. A similar result can be also found in
[
134
]
.

The wave function in (
83
) that is attributed to a classical light beam here, is known to satisfy a Schrödinger-like equation
[
55
,
84
,
7
,
8
,
13
,
154
,
139
,
38
]

i\frac{\partial\Uppsi}{\partial v}=\hat{H}\Uppsi,

(86)

which is sometimes referred to as the
paraxial wave equation
in Newtonian optics. This follows from the fact that in the paraxial regime, second derivative of the wave function, with respect to the axis parameter, is assumed to be small and the ordinary wave equation reduces to (
86
)
[
139
]
. In our case, the problem is not much different, as we consider only thin light bundles which are studied via the linear geodesic deviation equation. The geodesic deviation variables of the bundle are transformed by a matrix transformation,
\mathbf{T}
, whose analogue in the Newtonian paraxial optics is known as the
ABCD matrix
. Nevertheless, (
86
) does not capture genuine wave behavior in our case, as the assumptions of the geometric optics regime still apply.

In the next subsection, we identify what the paraxial wave equation (
86
) implies for null bundles. This analysis will be important for the following sections once we relate the wavization procedure to physical observables of a light beam.

4.3
Hydrodynamic analogy and the origin of the wave-like behavior

In order to demonstrate what kind of physical information (
86
) incorporates we use an analogy with the hydrodynamic evolution equations. A similar analogy has been considered since the early times of quantum mechanics. This formalism is known due to Madelung’s work
[
97
]
and it is often referred to as the
hydrodynamic interpretation
. In the quantum case, the hydrodynamic interpretation is mathematically equivalent to the well-known de Broglie-Bohm theory
[
35
,
20
,
21
]
. Madelung’s approach and de Broglie-Bohm theory differ ontologically in the sense that the former is a genuine statistical interpretation while the latter is often interpreted as a deterministic theory. For our classical wavization problem, there is no necessity for an “interpretation”. We will see in the following sections that those hydrodynamic evolution equations are closely related to the physically measurable quantities of a light beam without referring to any probabilistic framework.

Let us start investigating the paraxial wave equation by reconsidering the wave function that represents the null bundle. First, we write it in its
polar form
as

\Uppsi\left(\bm{\xi},v\right)=\mathscr{R}\exp{\left(i\mathscr{S}\right)},

(87)

where
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{S}
are
real
functions. Once
\Uppsi
is substituted in (
86
), one can decompose the paraxial wave equation into its pure imaginary and real parts. We present them respectively as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{R}}{\partial v}=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathscr{R}\bm{\nabla}^{\intercal}\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}+\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}\right)^{\intercal}\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{R}\right)+\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{R}\right)^{\intercal}\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}\right)\right],

(88)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{S}}{\partial v}=-\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}\right)^{\intercal}\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{\bm{\xi}}^{\intercal}\bm{\mathcal{R}}\mathbf{\bm{\xi}}-\frac{1}{2\mathscr{R}}\bm{\nabla}^{\intercal}\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{R}\right],

(89)

where
\bm{\nabla}=\partial/\partial\bm{\xi}
as in (
75
).

Let us now define

\mathscr{R}^{2}:=I=\left(\Uppsi^{\dagger}\Uppsi\right)\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}:=\mathbf{p}=p^{1}\vec{s_{1}}+p^{2}\vec{s_{2}},

(90)

and substitute
\mathscr{R}
and
\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}
in (
88
) and (
89
). Then, we can rewrite those equations respectively as

\frac{\partial I}{\partial v}+\bm{\nabla}^{\intercal}\left(I\,\mathbf{p}\right)=0,

(91)

and

\frac{\partial\mathscr{S}}{\partial v}=-\left(H\left(\bm{\xi},\mathbf{p}\right)+\mathcal{W}\right),\qquad

(92)

where

\mathcal{W}:=-\frac{1}{2\mathscr{R}}\bm{\nabla}^{\intercal}\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{R}.

(93)

We realize that the pure imaginary part, (
91
), of the paraxial wave equation corresponds to a continuity equation for
I
which represents the intensity of the beam. The real part, (
92
), corresponds to an equation similar to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation, (
71
), of the underlying linear geodesic deviation of the null bundle with an extra term,
\mathcal{W}
. Also , note that in (
92
), the Hamiltonian function,
H
, inputs
\mathbf{p}
instead of
\bm{\dot{\xi}}
. Following the hydrodynamic analogy, the
\mathbf{p}=\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}
term can be interpreted as the
representative
or
effective momenta
of superposed bundles in our work similar to streamline momenta.
With this, the associated equations of motion can be written as

\frac{d\bm{p}}{dv}=-\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathcal{V}+\bm{\nabla}\mathcal{W}\right),

(94)

due to (
89
) where
\mathcal{V}=-\mathbf{\bm{\xi}}^{\intercal}\bm{\mathcal{R}}\mathbf{\bm{\xi}}/2
is the ray bundle potential which was previously introduced in (
57
). Now, it is easy to see that if
\mathcal{W}\rightarrow 0
, then

\displaystyle\bm{p}\rightarrow\bm{\dot{\xi}},\qquad H\left(\bm{\xi},\mathbf{p}\right)\rightarrow H(\bm{\xi},\bm{\dot{\xi}}),\qquad\frac{d\bm{p}}{dv}=-\left(\bm{\nabla}\mathcal{V}+\bm{\nabla}\mathcal{W}\right)\rightarrow\ddot{\bm{\xi}}=\mathcal{R}\bm{\xi}.

(95)

This means that the additional potential term,
\mathcal{W}
, is the object that is responsible for the wave-like behavior of the superposed bundles. This is analogous to the case in the Madelung-de Broglie-Bohm formalism where the quantum potential is solely responsible for the quantum behavior of a given system.

At this point, we should also mention that the hydrodynamic analogy being a useful tool in extracting the wave-like properties of classical and quantum systems was also discussed in
[
117
]
. We believe this formalism is relevant in our problem as well since light bundles are represented by an effective stress-energy tensor of a null dust in the geometric optics limit. Most importantly, we will see in Sections
6.4.2
and
6.4.3
that the hydrodynamic analogy helps us in distinguishing light beams which can avoid caustics form the ones which can not, in Section
6
.

In the next section, we study light bundles initiated from a point source. We investigate the evolution of the light bundle wave function and compare its associated observables with the ones of the standard null bundle approach.

5
Point sources

5.1
Wave function and its physical relevance

We now follow the standard assumption of optics in cosmology. That is, we consider a point source which emits spherical waves and only a small section of the wavefront is accessible to an observer who is located at a far distance. One can also reverse the situation and assume that an observer shoots a light beam on the past null cone. In either of the cases, the congruence of null geodesics originates from a vertex point and the initial deviation vector components are zero, i.e.,
\bm{\xi}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
.
Accordingly, the initial wave function we assign to a bundle can be written as a delta distribution, i.e.,

\Uppsi_{0}\left(\bm{\xi}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\right)=C\delta\left(\bm{\xi}^{\prime}-\bm{\xi}_{0},v^{\prime}\right)=C\delta\left(\bm{\xi}^{\prime}-\mathbf{0},v^{\prime}\right),

(96)

where
C
is some constant.
Once we substitute this initial wave function into the integral transform (
83
),
we obtain the final wave function of the point source (
P.S.
) as

\Uppsi_{P.S.}\left(\bm{\xi},v\right)=\frac{C}{2\pi\left|{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}\right|^{1/2}}\exp\left({\frac{i}{2}\left[\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\bm{\xi}-\pi\right]}\right),

(97)

in its polar form
\Uppsi_{P.S.}\left(\bm{\xi},v\right)=\mathscr{R}_{P.S.}\exp{\left(i\mathscr{S}_{P.S.}\right)}
. Meaning, both the amplitude,

\mathscr{R}_{P.S.}=\frac{C}{2\pi\left|{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}\right|^{1/2}},

(98)

and the phase,

\mathscr{S}_{P.S.}={\frac{1}{2}\left[\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\bm{\xi}-\pi\right]},

(99)

are real in this form. In order to understand what the phase function
\mathscr{S}_{P.S.}
represents, we consider the generic bundle transformation. Let us recall that the geometry of a null bundle can be studied via two linearly independent solutions,
\vec{\xi}_{\perp}
and
\vec{\tilde{\xi}}_{\perp}
, of the null geodesic deviation equation projected on a local 2-dimensional screen. Namely, for

\mathbf{Q}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c}\xi{{}^{1}}&\,\,\tilde{\xi}{{}^{1}}\\
\xi{{}^{2}}&\,\,\tilde{\xi}{{}^{2}}\end{array}\right]\,\,{\rm{and}}\,\,\mathbf{P}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c}\dot{\xi}{{}_{1}}&\,\,\dot{\tilde{\xi}}{{}_{1}}\\
\dot{\xi}{{}_{2}}&\,\,\dot{\tilde{\xi}}{{}_{2}}\end{array}\right],

(100)

the wavefront curvature of the bundle can be obtained through
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Q}^{-1}
as outlined in Section
2.3
. It is easy to check that
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{P}
obey the same phase space transformation rules, (
61
), as the individual solutions
\bm{{\xi}}
and
\bm{\tilde{\xi}}
. That is,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}+\mathbf{D}\mathbf{P^{\prime}},

(101)

where the
2\times 2
matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}
are the sub-blocks of the ray bundle transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, as before. The primed objects indicate the initial values.
For the case of point sources we have
\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}=\mathbf{0}
, as the initial deviation vectors are zero at a vertex point. Then,

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Q}^{-1}=\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{-1}

(102)

gives the wavefront curvature of a bundle initiated from a point source. Note that this term is exactly equal to the phase function
\mathscr{S}_{P.S.}
in (
99
) in the so-called wavized case
**
**

**

Indeed, there is also a
\pi/2
phase shift which will discuss in a future work.
. We also recall that the deformation matrix,
\Gamma_{ab}
, relates the projected solutions of the geodesic deviation equation to their derivatives, i.e.,
\dot{\xi}_{a}=\langle\vec{s}_{a},\xi^{b}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{s}_{b}}\vec{k}\rangle=\Gamma_{ab}\xi^{b}
. Then, for null rays initiated from a point source,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}
in (
102
) is exactly equal to the deformation matrix given in (
24
). Its components give the expansion and shear scalars of the bundle through (
25
).

We now investigate what the amplitude,
\mathscr{R}_{P.S.}
, represents. From our previous discussions, we know that the determinant of
\mathbf{Q}
gives the cross-sectional area of a ray bundle. Then, for a bundle initiated from a point source, i.e.,
\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}=\mathbf{0}
, we have

\delta\mathcal{X}_{P.S.}={\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}={\rm{det}}\left(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}\right).

(103)

We remind that
\mathbf{P^{\prime}}
is the matrix through which one calculates the solid angles (See Section
2.5
.). Then, we have

\mathscr{R}_{P.S.}\sim{\left|{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}\right|^{-1/2}}\sim\delta\mathcal{X}_{P.S.}^{-1/2}\sim D_{L}^{-1},

(104)

where
D_{L}
is the luminosity distance as in (
74
). This means that the amplitude of the wave function is inversely proportional to the luminosity distance as expected.

In summary, for a wavized beam initiated from a point source, the phase function,
\mathscr{S}_{P.S.}
, gives the wavefront curvature matrix and it takes the same form as the one for a standard null bundle. The amplitude,
\mathscr{R}_{P.S.}
, of the wave function is inversely proportional to the square root of the cross-sectional area of the beam. In other words, it is inversely proportional to the luminosity distance.

5.2
The evolution equations and the power conservation

We now analyze the pure imaginary and the real parts of the paraxial wave equation for a beam initiated at a point source.
We start with the pure imaginary part which takes the form of a continuity equation, (
91
), for the intensity. For this, let us first write the intensity function for the point source case as,

I_{P.S.}=\Uppsi_{P.S.}^{\dagger}\Uppsi_{P.S.}=\left(\frac{C}{2\pi}\right)^{2}{\left|{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}\right|^{-1}}.

(105)

The term that is interpreted as the effective momenta in the continuity equation follows as

\mathbf{p}_{P.S.}:=\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}_{P.S.}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}\bm{\xi},

(106)

where
\mathscr{S}_{P.S.}
is given in (
99
). The second equality in the above follows from the fact that
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}=\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{-1}
is symmetric
*†
*†

*†

This is due to the symplectic symmetry
\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{D}^{\intercal}\mathbf{B}
of the ray bundle transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, presented in (
64
).
. Then it is easy to show that the continuity equation takes the form,

\frac{dI_{P.S.}}{dv}=-{\rm{Tr}}\left(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}\right)I_{P.S.}=-2\theta I_{P.S.},

(107)

where
\theta
is the expansion scalar
*‡
*‡

*‡

As

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}
is equal to the deformation rate matrix of the corresponding ray bundle, the trace of
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}
gives twice the expansion scalar through (
25
).
of the null bundle. We also realize that this continuity equation is equivalent to the evolution equation of the squared amplitude of the electromagnetic wave in the leading order approximation of the JWKB-like method. That is, (
107
) is equivalent to (
42
) of the geometric optics limit.

It is known that in quantum mechanics, the Schrödinger equation preserves the norm of a wave function which is normalized to one. In our case, it is the paraxial wave equation, (
86
), that preserves the non-unit norm. Namely,

\mathcal{P}=\int_{0}^{\bm{\xi}^{*}}\left(\Uppsi^{\dagger}\Uppsi\right)d^{2}\bm{\xi},

(108)

is invariant throughout the evolution. In our classical picture,
\mathcal{P}
corresponds to the integrated intensity over the cross-sectional area. It represents the power contained within a light beam.

In order to show that
\mathcal{P}
is conserved, we first consider the evolution equation for the cross-sectional area,
\delta\mathcal{X}_{P.S.}={\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}={\rm{det}}\left(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}\right)
, of the beam. Once we take its derivative along the propagation direction,
\vec{k}
, we obtain

\frac{d\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}\right)}{dv}=\rm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}\right)\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}\right),

(109)

by making use of Hamilton’s equations, (
65
), in the matrix form.
Then, we observe that the power is indeed conserved, i.e.,

\frac{d\mathcal{P}_{P.S.}}{dv}=\frac{dI_{P.S.}}{dv}\delta\mathcal{X}_{P.S.}+I_{P.S.}\frac{d\delta\mathcal{X}_{P.S.}}{dv}=0,

(110)

due to the evolution equations of intensity and the cross-sectional area, presented in (
107
) and (
109
) respectively. In other words, the imaginary part of the paraxial wave equation guarantees that the power is constant throughout the propagation. This means that the evolution equation of
I_{P.S.}
is a genuine continuity equation, rather than being a mere analogy. We should note that this result is also equivalent to the photon number conservation of the bundle which is sometimes referred to as the
area law
in the cosmological context as in (
44
).

Let us now look into the real part of the paraxial wave equation. First thing to check is the value of
\mathcal{W}
which is the object that differentiates the evolution equations of superposed light bundles from the Hamilton-Jacobi equation of a standard null bundle. By substituting the amplitude function
\mathscr{R}_{P.S.}=C/\left(2\pi\left|{\rm{det}}\mathbf{B}\right|\right)^{1/2}
in the definition of
\mathcal{W}
(
93
), we determine that
\mathcal{W}_{P.S.}=0
. This follows from the fact that the intensity of the wave is homogeneous on the observational screen and its value is same as the one of the electromagnetic field corresponding to the central null geodesic in the geometric optics limit.

Moreover, it is easy to show that the effective momenta,
\mathbf{p}_{P.S.}
, of the wavized beam given in (
106
) are indeed equal to
\bm{\dot{\xi}}=\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\bm{{\xi}}
which are the canonical momenta of the standard null bundle. Then, the real part of the paraxial wave equation is equal to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation, (
71
), of the ordinary thin null bundle formulation. Accordingly, the equations of motion of the wavized beam are same as the screen projected geodesic deviation equations,
\ddot{\bm{\xi}}=\mathcal{R}\bm{\xi}
.

We should note that this result is directly related to the evolution equation of the wavefront curvature matrix. As
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}
is equal to the deformation matrix, we expect it to satisfy the real, non-linear Riccati equation,

{\mathbf{\dot{\Gamma}}_{P.S.}}+\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}-\mathcal{R}=0.

(111)

We observe that the equation above is indeed satisfied once we make use of the matrix Hamiltonian (
65
) that govern the evolution of the ray bundle. Eventually, solving this evolution equation for
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{P.S.}
is equivalent to solving the Sachs optical equations of a standard bundle as we discussed in Section
2.3
.

We complete this section by reaching the following conclusion. In the case of a perfect point source, the evolution equations of a “wavized” bundle involve the same information as the evolution equations of a standard ray bundle. No new information can be gained through the so-called wavization procedure. This situation is analogous to the propagation of almost-spherical waves in the paraxial Newtonian optics. In the next section, we explore more interesting solutions of the wavization procedure in which non-trivial results are obtained.

6
Gaussian beams

The connection between the paraxial ray optics and the mathematical formulation of Gaussian beams was established around the 1960s in the Newtonian optics
[
85
,
84
,
7
,
8
]
. On the other hand, there is no consensus in the literature on a unique method to obtain a Gaussian beam profile starting from the standard ray theory
[
133
,
28
]
. The only common ground is the introduction of complex variables. For example, in the early works, it was stated that when the position and the direction of a ray take complex values, the curvature of the wavefront also becomes complex. This results in a transverse profile for the wave amplitude
[
8
,
39
,
9
]
. In that case, the Riccati equation that govern the evolution of the curvature matrix of the wavefront and which is real in the ray optics scenario becomes complex
[
28
,
18
,
19
]
. For practical applications, authors independently consider a complex refractive index, a complex wave vector and a complex eikonal in order to study the Gaussian beams
[
31
,
49
,
74
,
114
]
. When the complex geometric optics was developed, it was argued that the light follows trajectories in a genuinely complex space and the initial values of a trajectory take complex values in order to create the wave effects
[
82
,
90
,
19
]
. We should also note that a unified view was established recently through the introduction of a complex Maxwell stress-energy tensor in the Newtonian optics
[
112
]
.

The method we adapt in this work is aligned mostly with the works of Arnaud
[
8
]
and Cerveny
[
26
,
28
]
where the authors focus on those rays whose displacement vectors take complex values when measured from an axis. In our case, it is the relative dynamics of the central null geodesic and the outer-most geodesic that shapes the physical observables. Therefore, the location at which the ray pierces a transverse screen is obtained through the geodesic deviation vector. In this respect, we focus on the complex sum of two real solutions of the geodesic deviation equation.

In the following sections, we suggest a geometric construction which results in a Gaussian beam profile for a narrow beam initiated from a small, yet a finite sized source. The characteristic parameters of this beam depend on the curvature of the underlying spacetime rather than random processes.

6.1
On the complex wavefront curvature

For a generic beam, the amplitude of the intensity of light changes not only in the propagation direction but also in the transverse direction. For a Gaussian beam, the intensity is maximum at the center and it decays exponentially towards the edges of a
v=\rm{constant}
surface. In order to have such an exponential decay, one requires a complex curvature matrix,
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}+i\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
of the wavefront, where
\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
are real. To be more specific, the corresponding wave function of the beam should be in the form

\Uppsi=\frac{\kappa}{\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}\right)^{1/2}}\exp{\left(\frac{i}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}\bm{\xi}\right)}\exp{\left(\frac{-1}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\bm{\xi}\right)},

(112)

where
\kappa=\left(I_{0}\,{\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}\right)^{1/2}
is a constant with
I_{0}
being the initial intensity of the beam and
\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}
being the initial value of the complex matrix
\mathbf{Q}
.
Note that without the complex part,
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
, of the wavefront curvature matrix, the exponential decay of the amplitude is not possible. Here, we should also mention that
\bm{\xi}
is not representing the deviation vector of the outer-most geodesic of the bundle any longer. Rather, it is a variable on the 2-dimensional screen space. We also note that
{\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}
is independent of
\bm{\xi}
. Its specification will be clear in a while.

In the previous sections, we only considered those curvature matrices which are real. This was due to choosing real solutions of the geodesic deviation equation to construct
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{P}
matrices. In order to obtain the aforementioned complex curvature matrix,
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Q}^{-1}
, one needs to consider complex solutions, or as we do here, complex combinations of real solutions of the geodesic deviation equation. Let us write them explicitly as,

\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{Q_{R}}+i\mathbf{Q_{I}},\qquad{\rm{and}\qquad}\mathbf{P}:=\mathbf{\dot{Q}}=\mathbf{\dot{Q}_{R}}+i\mathbf{\dot{Q}_{I}}.

(113)

In general, one can consider any four linearly independent solutions to construct the complex
\mathbf{Q}
(and
\mathbf{P}
) matrices as long as certain criteria are satisfied.
To be specific, in order for a Gaussian beam to be well defined throughout the propagation, it has to fulfill the following requirements
[
124
]

i)

The matrix
\mathbf{Q}
should be regular for all values of the evolution parameter
v
. This means
{\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}\neq 0\,\forall\,v
,

ii)

\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
should be positive definite,

iii)

The complex curvature matrix,
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, should be symmetric. In other words, both
\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
should be symmetric matrices.

It is known that as long as the criteria above are satisfied at the initial point, they are satisfied throughout the evolution
[
28
]
.

6.2
Gaussian beams remain Gaussian

In order for the Gaussian wave function in (
112
) to be a solution of the paraxial wave (
86
) for each value of the affine parameter
v
, the solution has to preserve its form throughout the propagation. Then, one requires
[
85
,
8
]

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\left(\mathbf{C}+\mathbf{D}\mathbf{\Gamma^{\prime}}\right)\left(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Gamma^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}\left(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Gamma^{\prime}}\right),

(114)

between the initial and final values of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\mathbf{Q}
.
The relations above are known as the
ABCD law
in the Newtonian optics literature. Even though those results are a well-known, we provide a detailed proof of their derivation in
A
, for the completeness of the paper. We also would like to highlight that the relations in (
114
) hold due to the underlying symplectic symmetries of the ray bundle transformation matrix,
\mathbf{T}
.

6.3
The principal curvatures and the widths

Recall from Section
2.3
that for light bundles whose deviation vector is in orthogonal correspondence with its propagation vector, the principal curvatures of the wavefront are found through the eigenvalues of a real curvature matrix,
\bm{\Gamma}
. The wavefront, in that case, is defined by a surface which represents
both
the constant phase
and
the constant amplitude surfaces of a wave field. For Gaussian beams, the situation is different. Namely, the constant amplitude and the constant phase surfaces do not coincide. Accordingly, we need to define both the principal curvatures and the widths in order to characterize the geometry of the beam. This requires the simultaneous diagonalization of the real part,
\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}
, and the imaginary part,
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
, of the complex curvature matrix,
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Essentially, we are looking for a non-singular, real transformation that takes the Sachs basis into a possibly non-orthonormal basis with respect to which the curvature matrix,
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, is diagonalized. We require this in order for the principal curvatures and the widths to be consistently defined.

Simultaneous diagonalization of two real matrices is not a trivial problem. However, if those two matrices are symmetric and at least one of the matrices is positive definite, then the simultaneous diagonalization is possible
[
75
]
. In Section
6.1
, we mentioned that in order to have a well defined Gaussian beam, both
\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
have to be symmetric. In addition,
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
has to be positive definite. Thus, diagonalization of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is possible.

In
B
, we outline the set of transformations one needs to apply on
\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
in order to diagonalize them simultaneously. Essentially, we transform the Sachs basis,
\vec{s}
, into a new spatial, 2-dimensional, real screen basis,
\vec{e}
, via
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}\mathbf{e}

where
\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}
is orthonormal. Then, the Sachs basis components,
\bm{\xi}
, of the screen projected deviation vector are transformed via
\bm{\xi}=\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}\mathbf{r}

where
\mathbf{r}
is the position vector on the 2-dimensional screen in the new basis. The procedure presented in
B
allows us to put the wave function of a generic Gaussian beam in (
112
) into the following form,

\Uppsi=\frac{\kappa}{\left({\rm{det}\mathbf{Q}}\right)^{1/2}}\exp{\left(\frac{i}{2}\bm{r}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\Gamma_{R}}\bm{r}\right)}\exp{\left(\frac{-1}{2}\bm{r}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\Gamma_{I}}\bm{r}\right)}.

(115)

Here,

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\Gamma_{R}}=\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}^{\intercal}\Gamma_{R}\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c}K_{1}&\,\,0\\
0&K_{2}\end{array}\right],\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\Gamma_{I}}=\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}^{\intercal}\Gamma_{I}\mathbf{\mathcal{M}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c}W_{1}^{-2}&\,\,0\\
0&W_{2}^{-2}\end{array}\right],

with
\{K_{1}(v),K_{2}(v)\}
being the principal curvatures of the Gaussian beam and
\{W_{1}(v),W_{2}(v)\}
represent its widths given at some value of
v
.

6.4
Finite sources

6.4.1
The geometric construction:

This section presents the core of the paper where we suggest a method to geometrically construct a Gaussian beam. With this construction, the Gaussian beam fulfills the welldefinedness criteria (i)-(iii) of Section
6.1
. In addition, it allows one to obtain the physical parameters of the beam explicitly. We should note that the geometric construction we propose here formulates a Gaussian beam which initiates from a finite source. However, it should be noted that this is not a unique way of formulating a Gaussian beam and one might consider other options for different physical scenarios.

(a)

Two null congruences,
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{1}}}}
, and
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{2}}}}
, are initiated from a finite source,
S
, and represented by dashed lines. The Gaussian beam is obtained through their complex sum and it is sketched with solid lines here.

\vec{s}_{2}
\vec{s}_{1}
\vec{e}_{1}
\vec{e}_{2}
\vec{\bm{\tilde{\zeta}}}_{\perp}
\vec{\bm{\zeta}}_{\perp}
\vec{\bm{\tilde{\eta}}}_{\perp}
\vec{\bm{\eta}}_{\perp}

(b)

Projected solutions of the geodesic deviation equation for congruences
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{1}}}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{2}}}}
.

Figure 4
:
The sketch of Gaussian beams and the transverse screen.

Let us begin with considering two null congruences,
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{1}}}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{2}}}}
, which share the same central null geodesic with a tangent vector
\vec{k}
(See Figure (
4(a)
).). We denote the Sachs basis components of two linearly independent, real solutions of the geodesic deviation equation of
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{1}}}}
as
\bm{\eta}
and
\bm{\tilde{\eta}}
. Similarly, the Sachs basis components of any two linearly independent, real solutions belonging to
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{2}}}}
are denoted as
\bm{\zeta}
and
\bm{\tilde{\zeta}}
(See Figure (
4(b)
).). We use the deviation vectors of the first congruence,
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{1}}}}
, to construct the matrix
\mathbf{Q_{R}}
and the ones of the second congruence,
\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{2}}}}
, to construct
\mathbf{Q_{I}}
, i.e.,

\mathbf{Q_{R}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c}\eta{{}^{1}}&\,\,\tilde{\eta}{{}^{1}}\\
\eta{{}^{2}}&\,\,\tilde{\eta}{{}^{2}}\end{array}\right],\qquad\mathbf{Q_{I}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c}\zeta{{}^{1}}&\,\,\tilde{\zeta}{{}^{1}}\\
\zeta{{}^{2}}&\,\,\tilde{\zeta}{{}^{2}}\end{array}\right].

(120)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q_{R}}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{R}}+\mathbf{B}{\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{R}}},\qquad\mathbf{Q_{I}}=\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{I}}}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{I}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{P_{R}}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{R}}+\mathbf{D}{\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{R}}},\qquad\mathbf{P_{I}}=\mathbf{C}{\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{I}}}+\mathbf{D}\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{I}}.

Next, we choose different initial conditions for the solutions of the geodesic deviation equation for each congruence. Namely,

\displaystyle\{\bm{\eta^{\prime}}\neq\mathbf{0},\bm{\dot{\eta}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{0}\}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\{\bm{\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}}\neq\mathbf{0},\bm{\dot{\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}}}=\mathbf{0}\}\qquad{\rm{for}}\,\,\,\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{1}}}},

\displaystyle\{\bm{\zeta^{\prime}}=\mathbf{0},\bm{\dot{\zeta}^{\prime}}\neq\mathbf{0}\}\qquad{\rm{and}}\qquad\{\bm{\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{0},\bm{\dot{\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}}}\neq\mathbf{0}\}\qquad{\rm{for}}\,\,\,\mathscr{C}_{\scalebox{0.5}{{\textcircled{2}}}}.

(121)

We will see in a while that having a complex
\mathbf{Q}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{Q_{R}}+i\mathbf{Q_{I}}
as above allows us to extend the formalism from point sources to finite sources.

Next, we calculate the complex curvature matrix as

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{PQ}^{-1}=\left(\mathbf{P_{R}}+i\mathbf{P_{I}}\right)\left(\mathbf{Q_{R}}+i\mathbf{Q_{I}}\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}=\left(\mathbf{C}{\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{R}}}+i\mathbf{D}\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{I}}\right)\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{R}}+i\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{I}}\right)^{-1},

(122)

with the initial conditions (
121
), i.e.,
\{\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{R}}=\mathbf{0},\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{I}}=\mathbf{0}\}
.
In addition, the initial values
\mathbf{Q^{\prime}_{R}}
and
\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{I}}
are chosen in such a way that they satisfy
\mathbf{Q_{R}^{\prime}}={W_{0}}^{2}\mathbf{I_{2}}\mathbf{P^{\prime}_{I}}
, where
W_{0}
corresponds to the initial width of the beam that is sometimes referred to as the
waist
or the
spot size
. Such a choice of initial conditions allows one to study a beam which propagates starting from its waist. It incorporates information about the initial area of a finite size from which light rays are initiated.
Substitution of the initial conditions into the complex curvature matrix in (
122
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{G.B.}=\left(\mathbf{\tilde{C}}+i{\mathbf{D}}\right)\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}}+i{\mathbf{B}}\right)^{-1},

(123)

where we write
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}={W_{0}}^{2}{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathbf{\tilde{C}}={W_{0}}^{2}{\mathbf{C}}
for simpler notation.
Then, the curvature matrix can be written in the form
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{G.B.}=\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}+i\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
with
*§
*§

*§

In order to find the real and the pure imaginary parts of
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{G.B.}
, we first replace
\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}}+i{\mathbf{B}}\right)^{-1}
in (
123
) with
\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-i{\mathbf{B}}\right)^{\intercal}\left[\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}}+i{\mathbf{B}}\right)\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-i{\mathbf{B}}\right)^{\intercal}\right]^{-1}.

Then, we use the symplectic properties
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}
and
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}-\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{I_{2}}
of the ray bundle transformation matrix given in (
64
).

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}=\left[\mathbf{\tilde{C}}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}^{\intercal}+\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}\right]\left[\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}^{\intercal}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}\right]^{-1},

(124)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}={W_{0}}^{2}\left[\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}^{\intercal}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}\right]^{-1}.

(125)

Note that all of the criteria required for a well-defined Gaussian beam propagation are fulfilled in this case. Meaning,

i)

\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}+i\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}
is regular everywhere,

ii)

\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
is positive definite,

iii)

\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
which are respectively given in (
124
) and (
125
) are symmetric matrices.

The proof of those results are presented in
C
.

6.4.2
Evolution equations and the power conservation:

We now study the evolution equations of the Gaussian beam presented in the previous subsection.
Let us recall that in the case of a beam initiated from a point source, the wavization procedure does not bring any new information to the standard null bundle evolution equations. Here, we identify certain wave-like properties of a Gaussian beam while keeping track of the differences between beams of point sources and finite sources.

In order to investigate the Gaussian beam evolution within the hydrodynamic analogy, we have to put the Gaussian wave function in (
112
) into its polar form. However, it is not immediately obvious whether this is possible for a generic Gaussian wave function, as the determinant of
\mathbf{Q}
that appears inside the square root is complex. On the other hand, the geometric construction we presented in Section
6.4.1
allows us to perform the following manipulations.

We start with decomposing
{\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}^{1/2}
, that appears on the denominator of the Gaussian wave function (
112
), into its real and pure imaginary parts. For this, we first substitute the initial conditions mentioned earlier into
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Q^{\prime}}+i\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}
. Then, its determinant follows as
{\rm{det}}\mathbf{Q}={\rm{det}}\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}+i\mathbf{B}}\right){\rm{det}}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}
.
In that case, the Gaussian wave function can be rewritten as

\Uppsi=\frac{\beta}{\left({\rm{det}}\left[\mathbf{\tilde{A}+i\mathbf{B}}\right]\right)^{1/2}}\exp{\left(\frac{i}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}\bm{\xi}\right)}\exp{\left(\frac{-1}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\bm{\xi}\right)}.

(126)

Here, the
\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}\right)^{-1/2}
is absorbed in the constant
\kappa
of (
112
), i.e.,
\beta=\kappa\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{P^{\prime}}\right)^{-1/2}=I_{0}^{1/2}W_{0}^{2}
. Now, the problem reduces to finding the real and the pure imaginary parts of
({\rm{det}}[\mathbf{\tilde{A}+i\mathbf{B}}])^{1/2}
.
For this, we use the fact that there exists a unitary matrix
*¶
*¶

*¶

In
C
, we reminded the Iwasawa pre-decomposition via which the symplectic matrix,
\mathbf{T}
, can be factorized into three portions: the shearing matrix, the pure magnifier and the fractional Fourier transformer. We highlight that the unitary matrix in (
127
) is a slight modification of the unitary matrix, (
191
), that is associated with the fractional Fourier transformer part of
\mathbf{T}
. They are equal when the initial width is set to 1.
,

\mathbf{u}=\left[\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}^{\intercal}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}\right]^{-1/2}\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}}+i\mathbf{B}\right),

(127)

associated with
\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}+i\mathbf{B}}\right)
. It follows from the definition of the unitary matrices that,
\mathbf{u}
can be uniquely factorized as a product of two unitary matrices, i.e.,
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{{u}_{1}}\mathbf{{u}_{2}}
, where
\mathbf{u_{1}}=\exp{\left(i\alpha/2\right)}\mathbf{I_{2}}
and
\mathbf{u_{2}}\in
SU(2). Then, taking the determinant of both sides of (
127
) gives us
*∥
*∥

*∥

Here we use (
125
), and the symplectic condition
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}=\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}^{\intercal}\right)^{\intercal}
given in (
64
).

{\rm{det}}\left(\mathbf{\tilde{A}+i\mathbf{B}}\right)=W_{0}^{2}\exp{\left(i\alpha\right)}\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\right)^{-1/2}.

(128)

Finally, we substitute equation above into (
126
) and we obtain the Gaussian wave function in its polar form as,

\Uppsi_{G.B.}=\mathscr{R}_{G.B.}\exp{\left(i\mathscr{S}_{G.B.}\right)},

(129)

where

\mathscr{R}_{G.B.}=\frac{I_{0}^{1/2}W_{0}}{{\rm{det}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}^{-1/4}}\exp{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\bm{\xi}\right)},

(130)

and

\mathscr{S}_{G.B.}=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}\bm{\xi}-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

(131)

Let us now investigate the physical observables associated with the Gaussian beam. We start with the intensity profile,

I_{G.B.}=\Uppsi_{G.B.}^{\dagger}\Uppsi_{G.B.}=\frac{I_{0}W_{0}^{2}\exp{\left(-\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\bm{\xi}\right)}}{{\rm{det}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}^{-1/2}}.

(132)

The first thing we realize is that as opposed to the intensity profile,
I_{P.S.}=I_{P.S.}(v)
, of a point source given in (
105
), the Gaussian beam intensity,
I_{G.B.}(\bm{\xi},v)
, changes along the transverse plane. Once we integrate the Gaussian beam intensity in (
132
) along the transverse plane, we find the power contained in the Gaussian beam, i.e.,

\mathcal{P}_{G.B.}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}I_{G.B}d^{2}\bm{\xi}=\mathcal{P}_{0},

(133)

where
\mathcal{P}_{0}=I_{0}\left(\pi W_{0}^{2}\right)
is a constant that gives the initial power contained within the beam.
Thus, similar to the bundle initiated from a point source, the power is conserved within our Gaussian beam initiated at a finite extend. This is the consequence of defining the beam in the geometric optics limit.

Next, we look into the evolution equations and we start our analysis with the imaginary part of the so-called paraxial wave equation. For this, we first calculate
\mathbf{p}_{G.B.}
which is interpreted as the representative effective momenta of the beam. Through its definition in (
90
) it follows as

\mathbf{p}_{G.B.}=\bm{\nabla}\mathscr{S}_{G.B.}=\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}\bm{\xi}.

(134)

Substitution of
I_{G.B.}
and
\mathbf{p}_{G.B.}
into the imaginary part of the paraxial wave equation, (
91
), gives an evolution equation for
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
as,

\mathbf{\dot{\Gamma}_{I}}+\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}+\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}=0.

(135)

Note that this equation is equivalent to,

\frac{d\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\right)}{dv}=-2\left({{\rm{Tr}}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{R}}\right)\left({\rm{det}}\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}\right).

(136)

As the imaginary part of the wavefront curvature,
\mathbf{\Gamma_{I}}
